Meaningfulness and Genericity in a Subsuming Framework
Delia Kesner, Victor Arrial, Giulio Guerrieri

Abstract
This paper studies the notion of meaningfulness for a unifying framework called dBang -calculus, which subsumes both call-by-name ( dCBN ) and call-by-value ( dCBV ). We first characterize meaningfulness in dBang by means of typability and inhabitation in an associated non-idempotent intersection type system previously proposed in the literature. We validate the proposed notion of meaningfulness by showing two properties (1) consistency of the theory ℋ \mathcal{H} equating meaningless terms and (2) genericity, stating that meaningless subterms have no bearing on the significance of meaningful terms. The theory ℋ \mathcal{H} is also shown to have a unique consistent and maximal extension. Last but not least, we show that the notions of meaningfulness and genericity in the literature for dCBN and dCBV are subsumed by the respectively ones proposed here for the dBang -calculus.

1
Introduction

A common line of research in logic and theoretical computer science is
to find unifying frameworks that subsume different paradigms, systems
or calculi. Examples are call-by-push-value
[
50
,
51
]
,
polarized system LU
[
42
]
, linear
calculi
[
53
,
54
,
65
]
,
bang-calculus
[
35
,
36
,
20
,
21
]
,
system L
[
58
,
32
]
, ecumenical
systems
[
62
]
, monadic calculus
[
56
,
57
]
, and
others
[
64
,
37
,
66
]
.

The relevance of these unifying frameworks lies in the range of
properties and models they encompass. Finding
unifying and
simple primitives, tools and techniques
to reason about properties of
different systems is challenging, and provides
a deeper and more
abstract understanding of these properties. The advantages of this
kind of approach are numerous, for instance the
several-for-one
deal
: study a property in a unifying framework gives
appropriate intuitions and hints for free
for all the subsumed systems. The aim of this paper is to go beyond
the state of the art in a framework subsumig the
call-by-name

and
call-by-value
evaluation mechanisms, by unifying the
notions of
meaningful
(and
meaningless
) programs.

Call-by-name and call-by-value.
Every programming
language implements a particular evaluation strategy, specifying when
and how parameters are evaluated during function calls. For example,
in call-by-value (
CBV
), the argument is evaluated before being
passed to the function, while in call-by-name (
CBN
) the argument
is passed immediately to the function body, so that it may never be
evaluated, or may be re-evaluated several times. These models of
computation serve as the basis for many theoretical and practical
studies in programming languages and proof assistants, such as OCaml,
Haskell, Coq, Isabelle, etc.

The
CBN
strategy has garnered significant attention in the
literature and is generally perceived as well-established. In
contrast, the
CBV
strategy has received limited attention.
Indeed, despite their similarities,
CBN
and
CBV
strategies
have predominantly been studied independently, leading to a fragmented
research. This approach not only duplicates research efforts—once for

CBN
and again for
CBV
— but also generally results in ad-hoc
methods for dealing with the
CBV
case that are naively adapted
from the
CBN
one.

Understanding the (logical) duality between
CBN
and
CBV
(
e.g.

[
31
]
) marked a significant step towards properly
unifying these models. It paved the way for the emergence of
Call-by-Push-Value (
CBPV
), a unifying framework introduced by
P.B. Levy
[
50
]
which
subsumes
, among others,

CBN
and
CBV
denotational and operational semantics thanks
to the distinction between
computations
and
values
,
according to the slogan “a value is, a computation does”. This
framework attracts growing attention: proving advanced properties of a
single
unifying paradigm
, and subsequently instantiate them for
a
wide range
of computational models.

The distant
BANG
-calculus.

Drawing inspiration from Girard’s Linear Logic (LL)
[
41
]

and the interpretation
[
35
]
of
CBPV
into LL, Ehrhard
and Guerrieri
[
36
]
introduced an (untyped)
restriction of
CBPV
, named
BANG
-calculus, already capable of
subsuming both
CBN
and
CBV
. It is obtained by enriching the

\lambda
-calculus with two modalities
\oc
and its dual
{\tt der}
.
The modality
\oc
actually plays a twofold role: it freezes the
evaluation of subterms (called
thunk
in
CBPV
), and it
marks what can be duplicated or erased during evaluation (
i.e.
copied
an arbitrary number of times, including zero). The modality
{\tt der}

annihilates the effect of
\oc
, effectively restoring computation and
eliminating duplicability. Embedding
CBN
or
CBV
into the

BANG
-calculus simply consists in decorating
\lambda
-terms with

\oc
and
{\tt der}
, thereby forcing one model of computation or the
other one. Thanks to these elementary modalities and embeddings, the
Bang Calculus eases the identification of shared behaviors and
properties of
CBN
and
CBV
, encompassing both syntactic and
semantic aspects of them.

The original
BANG
-calculus
[
36
]
uses
some permutation rules, similar
to the ones used in
[
63
,
27
]
, that unveil
hidden redexes and unblock reductions that otherwise would be stuck.
These permutation rules make the calculus
adequate
,
preventing that some normal forms are observationally equivalent to
non-terminating terms. A major
drawback is that the resulting combined reduction is not
confluent (Page 6 in
[
36
]
). As an alternative,
the
distant Bang calculus
(
dBang
)
[
20
,
21
]

is both adequate and confluent. This is achieved by
enriching the syntax with
explicit substitutions
, in the vein of
Accattoli and Kesner’s linear substitution
calculus
[
6
,
8
,
1
,
2
]

(generalizing in turn Milner’s calculus
[
55
,
47
]
),
thanks to rewrite rules that act
at a distance
, so that permutation
rules are no longer needed.

In this paper, we focus on
dBang
, and its relations with

dCBN

[
8
,
1
]
and

dCBV

[
10
]
, which are
distant

adequate variants of the
CBN
and
CBV
-calculi. This unifying
framework has proven fruitful, subsuming numerous
dCBN
and

dCBV
properties through their associated embedding, as for
instance big step semantics: evaluating the result from the

dCBN
/
dCBV
embedding of a given program
t

with the
dBang
model actually corresponds to the embedding
of the result of evaluating the original program
t
with the

dCBN
/
dCBV
model. In other words,

dBang
is a language that breaks down the
dCBN
and
dCBV
paradigms into elementary primitives.

Let us now review the state of the art by discussing some advanced
properties of programming languages that have been studied in the
literature by using the unifying approach
dBang
.
Some of these results, including this work, strongly rely on
semantical tools such as quantitative types. To ensure clarity
regarding the state of the art, let us briefly discuss in first place
the main ideas behind quantitative types.

Quantitative Type Systems.

Intersection type
systems

[
28
,
29
]
increase the typability
power of simply typed
\lambda
-terms by introducing a new

intersection
type constructor
\wedge
, which is, in
principle, associative, commutative and
idempotent
(
i.e.

\sigma\wedge\sigma=\sigma
). Intersection types allow terms to
have different types simultaneously,
e.g.
a term
t
has type
\sigma\wedge\tau
whenever
t
has both the type
\sigma
and the type

\tau
. They turns out to constitute a powerful tool to reason about

qualitative
properties of programs. For example, different
notions of normalization can be characterized using intersection
types
[
61
,
30
]
, in the sense that a
term
t
is typable in a given system if and only if
t
is
normalizing (as a consequence, typability in these systems is
undecidable). Removing idempotence
[
40
,
33
]
gives
rise to
non-idempotent
type systems for the

\lambda
-calculus where a term of type
\sigma\land\sigma\land\tau
can be seen as a resource that is used exactly once as a data
of type
\tau
and twice as a data of type
\sigma
. Interestingly,
such type systems do not only provide qualitative characterizations of
different operational properties, but also
quantitative
ones:

e.g.
a term
t
is still typable if and only if
t
is normalizing, but
additionally, any type derivation of
t
gives an
upper bound

to the execution time for
t
(the number of steps to reach a normal
form)
[
34
]
. These upper bounds can be further refined
into
exact measure
through the use of
tight
non-idempotent typing systems
, as pioneered by
[
3
]
.

State of the Art.

This paper contributes to a broader initiative aimed at consolidating
the theory of
dCBN
and
dCBV
, by unifying them
into
dBang
. Several results have already been factorized
and generalized in this framework, we now revisit some of them.

In
[
43
]
it is shown that the interpretation of
a term
t
in any denotational model of
CBN
/
CBV
obtained
from LL is included in the interpretation of the
CBN
/
CBV
translation of
t
in any denotational model of
BANG
obtained
from LL. The reverse inclusion also holds for
CBN
but not for

CBV
. In particular, these results apply to
typability
in a
non-idempotent intersection type system inspired by LL. Indeed, typing
is preserved by Girard’s translations, meaning that if a term is
typable in the
CBN
/
CBV
type system, then its

CBN
/
CBV
translation is typable in the type system

\mathcal{B}
for
BANG
, using the same types. The converse also holds for
CBN
but not for
CBV
. In

[
20
,
21
]
, the

CBV
typing system is modified so that the reverse implication also
holds. Moreover an extension of Girard’s
CBN
translation to

dCBN
and a
new

CBV
translation to

dCBV
are proposed. Similar typing preservation results
have been obtained in
[
49
]
for the translations
in
[
20
,
21
]
, but
for the more precise notion of tight typing
introduced in
[
3
]
.

Retrieving
dynamic
properties from
BANG
into
CBN
and

CBV
turns out to be a more intricate task, especially in their

adequate
(distant)
variant
[
20
,
38
,
21
]
.

In
[
43
]
it is also shown that
CBN
and

CBV
can be simulated by
reduction
in
BANG
through
Girard’s original translations. But the
CBV
translation fails to
preserve
normal forms
, as some
CBV
normal forms
translate to reducible terms in
BANG
. This issue is solved in

dBang

[
20
,
21
]
,
thanks to the
new

CBV
translation to
dCBV
mentioned before. In the end, reductions
and
normal forms are
preserved by both the
CBN
and the new
CBV
translations.

Even if
dCBN
and
dCBV
can be both simulated by

reduction
in
dBang
, the converse, known as

reverse simulation
, fails: a
dBang
reduction
sequence from a term in the image of the
dCBV
embedding
may not correspond to a valid reduction sequence in
dCBV
.
Yet another new
dCBV
translation is proposed in

[
13
]
so that simulation and reverse
simulation are now verified.

Another major contribution concerns the
inhabitation
problem:
given an environment
\Gamma
(a type assignment for variables) and a
type
\sigma
, decide whether there is a term
t
that can be typed
with
\sigma
under the environment
\Gamma
. While inhabitation was
shown
[
67
]
to be
undecidable
in
CBN
for
idempotent intersection type systems, it turns out to be

decidable

[
22
,
25
]
in the
non-idempotent setting. Decidability of the inhabitation problem leads
to the development of automatic tools for type-based
program
synthesis

[
52
,
19
]
, whose goal is to
construct a program —the term
t
— that
satisfies some high-level formal specification —expressed as a type

\sigma
with some assumptions described by the environment
\Gamma
.
It has been proved in
[
12
]
that the
algorithms deciding the inhabitation problem for
dCBN
and

dCBV
can be inferred from the corresponding one for

dBang
, thus providing a unified solution to this
relevant problem.

Meaningfulness and Genericity.
In this work, we aim
to unify the notions of meaningfulness and genericity in

dCBN
and
dCBV
so as to derive them from the
respective ones in
dBang
.

A naive approach to set a semantics for the pure untyped

\lambda
-calculus is to define the meaning of a
\beta
-normalizing

\lambda
-term as its normal form, and equating all
\lambda
-terms
that do not
\beta
-normalize. The underlying idea is that, as

\beta
-reduction represents evaluation and a normal form stands for
its outcome, all non-
\beta
-normalizing
\lambda
-terms are
considered as meaningless. However, this simplistic approach is
flawed, as thoroughly discussed in
[
18
]
. For example,
any
\lambda
-theory equating all non-
\beta
-normalizing
\lambda
-terms
is inherently inconsistent —it effectively equates all

\lambda
-terms, not just the meaningless ones!

Alternatively, during the 70s, Wadsworth

[
68
,
69
]
and Barendregt

[
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
]
showed
that the meaningful (
CBN
)
\lambda
-terms can be identified with
the
solvable
ones. Solvability is defined in a rather technical
way: a
\lambda
-term
t
is
solvable
if there is a special
kind of context, called
head
context
{\tt H}
, sending
t
to
the identity function
\mathtt{I}=\lambda z.z
, meaning that
{\tt H}\!\left<t\right>

\beta
-reduces to
\mathtt{I}
. Roughly, a solvable

\lambda
-term
t
may be divergent, but its diverging subterms can be
eliminated by supplying the right arguments to
t
via an appropriate
interaction with a suitable head context
{\tt H}
. For instance, in

{\tt CBN}
,
x\Omega
is divergent but solvable using the head context

{\tt H}=(\lambda x.\diamond)(\lambda y.\mathtt{I})
. It turns out that

unsolvable

\lambda
-terms constitutes a strict subset of the
non-
\beta
-normalizing ones.
Moreover, the smallest
\lambda
-theory that equates all unsolvable

\lambda
-terms is
consistent
(
i.e.
it does not equate all
terms). In Barendregt’s book
[
18
]
, theses results
relies on a keystone property known as
genericity
, which
states that meaningless subterms are computationally irrelevant —in
the sense that they do not play any role— in the evaluation of

\beta
-normalizing terms. Formally, if
t
is
unsolvable
and

{\tt C}\!\left<t\right>

\beta
-reduces to
some

\beta
-normal term
u
for some context
{\tt C}
, then
{\tt C}\!\left<s\right>

\beta
-reduces to
u
for
every

\lambda
-term
s
. This property stands as a fool guard that the
choice of meaningfulness is adequate.

Meaningfulness was also studied for first order rewriting
systems
[
45
]
and other strategies of the

\lambda
-calculus
[
64
]
. Notably, finding the correct
notion of meaningfulness for
CBV
has been a
challenge
[
4
,
14
]
.
Similarly, a notable extension of the
dCBN
was
studied
[
23
]
in the framework of a

\lambda
-calculus equipped with pattern matching for pairs. The use
of different data structures in the language —functions and pairs—
makes the meaningfulness problem more challenging. Indeed, it was
shown that meaningfulness cannot be characterized only by means of
typability alone, as in
CBN
and
CBV
, but also requires some
additional conditions stated in terms of the inhabitation problem
mentioned before. This result for the
\lambda
-calculus with patterns
inspired the characterization of meaningfulness that we provide in
this paper. Genericity for
dCBN
and the more subtle case
of
dCBV
was recently proved
in
[
14
]
.

Our Contributions.
We first define meaningfulness
for
dBang
, and provide a characterization by means of
typability
and
inhabitation. As a second contribution, we
validate this notion of meaningfulness twofold: meaningless terms
enjoy genericity, and the theory
\mathcal{H}
obtained by equating
all the meaningless terms is consistent. Moreover, we show that

\mathcal{H}
admits a unique maximal consistent extension. Last but
not least, as a third contribution, we show that the notions of
meaningfulness in the literature for
dCBN
and

dCBV
are subsumed by the one proposed here for

dBang
. We also obtained genericity for
dCBN
and
dCBV
as a consequence of the genericity property for

dBang
.

Roadmap.

Section

2
recalls
dBang
and its quantitative type system
\mathcal{B}
.

Section

3
defines meaningfulness for

dBang
, and characterizes it in terms of typability and
inhabitation in the type system
\mathcal{B}
.

Section

4
addresses genericity, while

Section

5
establishes a precise relationship between
meaningless and genericity in
dCBN
/
dCBV
and
their corresponding notions in
dBang
.
Section

6

discusses future and related work and concludes.

2
The
dBang
-Calculus

2.1
Syntax and Operational Semantics

We introduce here the term syntax of the
dBang
-calculus

[
20
,
21
]
.
Given a countably infinite set
\mathcal{X}
of variables
x,y,z,\dots
, the set of terms
\Lambda_{\oc}
is given by the following
inductive definition:

\textbf{(Terms)}\;\;t,u,s\;\coloneqq\;x\in\mathcal{X}\;|\;t\,u\;|\;\lambda x.t\;|\;\oc t\;|\;{\tt der}\!\left(t\right)\;|\;t[x\backslash u]

The set
\Lambda_{\oc}
includes
\lambda
-terms (
variables

x
,
abstractions

\lambda x.t
and
applications

tu
) as
well as three additional constructors: a
closure

t[x\backslash u]
representing a pending
explicit substitution
(ES)

[x\backslash u]
on a term
t
, a
bang

\oc t
to freeze the
execution of
t
, and a
dereliction

{\tt der}\!\left(t\right)
to fire again the
frozen term
t
. The
argument
of an application

t\,u
(resp. a closure
t[x\backslash u]
) is the subterm
u
.
From now on, we set
I\coloneqq\lambda z.z
,
\Delta\coloneqq\lambda x.x\oc x
, and
\Omega\coloneqq\Delta\oc\Delta
.

Abstractions
\lambda x.t
and closures
t[x\backslash u]
bind the
variable
x
in the term
t
.
Free
and

bound
variables are defined as expected, in particular

{\tt fv}(\lambda x.t)\coloneqq{\tt fv}(t)\setminus\{x\}
and

{\tt fv}(t[x\backslash u])\coloneqq{\tt fv}(u)\cup({\tt fv}(t)\setminus\{x\})
. The usual notion of
\alpha
-conversion

[
18
]
is extended to
\Lambda_{\oc}
,
and terms are identified up to
\alpha
-conversion. We denote by

t\{x\backslash u\}
the usual (capture avoiding) meta-level substitution of
the term
u
for all free occurrences of the variable
x
in the
term
t
.

The set of
list contexts

(\mathtt{L})
,
surface contexts

(\mathtt{S})
and
full contexts

(\mathtt{F})
, can be seen as terms containing exactly one

hole

\diamond
, they are inductively defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathtt{L}&\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{L}[x\backslash t]\\
\mathtt{S}&\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{S}\,t\;|\;t\,\mathtt{S}\;|\;\lambda x.\mathtt{S}\;|\;{\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{S}\right)\;|\;\mathtt{S}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash\mathtt{S}]\\
\mathtt{F}&\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{F}\,t\;|\;t\,\mathtt{F}\;|\;\lambda x.\mathtt{F}\;|\;{\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{F}\right)\;|\;\mathtt{F}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash\mathtt{F}]\;|\;\oc\mathtt{F}\end{array}

List contexts and surface contexts are special cases of
full contexts. The hole can occur everywhere in full contexts,
while it is forbidden in surface contexts under a
\oc
. For
example,
y\,(\lambda x.\diamond)
is a surface context
hence a full context, while
(\oc\diamond)[x\backslash\mathtt{I}]
is a full context but not a surface one. We write
\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>
for the
term obtained by replacing the hole in
\mathtt{F}
by the term
t
.

The following
rewrite rules
are the base components of the
reductions of
dBang
. Any term having the shape of the
left-hand side of one of these three rules is called a
redex
.

\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>u\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}\mathtt{L}\!\left<t[x\backslash u]\right>\qquad t[x\backslash\mathtt{L}\!\left<\oc u\right>]\mapsto_{{\tt s}\oc}\mathtt{L}\!\left<t\{x\backslash u\}\right>\qquad{\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{L}\!\left<\oc t\right>\right)\mapsto_{{\tt s}\oc}\mathtt{L}\!\left<t\right>

Rule
{\tt dB}
(resp.
{\tt s}\oc
) is assumed to be capture
free: no free variable of
u
(resp.
t
) is captured by the list
context
\mathtt{L}
. The rule
{\tt dB}
fires a standard

\beta
-redex and generates an ES. The rule
{\tt s}\oc
operates a
substitution provided its argument is a bang. The rule
{\tt d}\oc

opens a bang. All these rewrite rules act
at a
distance

[
6
,
8
,
2
]
:
the main constructors involved in the rule can be separated by a
finite—possibly empty—list context
\mathtt{L}
of ES. This
mechanism unblocks redexes that otherwise would be stuck,
e.g.

(\lambda x.x)[y\backslash w]\oc z\!\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}\!x[x\backslash\oc z][y\backslash w]
fires a
\beta
-redex where
\mathtt{L}=\diamond[y\backslash w]
is the list context in between the function

\lambda x.x
and the argument
\oc z
.

The
surface reduction

\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}
is the surface closure of the three rewrite rules
{\tt dB}
,
{\tt s}\oc

and
{\tt d}\oc
,
i.e.

\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}
only fires redexes in
surface contexts (not under bang). Similarly, the

full reduction

\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}}
is the full closure of
the three rewrite rules, so that
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}}
reduces under full
contexts and thus the bang looses its freezing behavior. For example,

(\lambda x.\oc{\tt der}\!\left(\oc x\right))\oc y\;\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}\;(\oc{\tt der}\!\left(\oc x\right))[x\backslash\oc y]\;\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}\;\oc({\tt der}\!\left(\oc y\right))\;\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}}\;\oc y

The first two steps are also
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}}
-steps, the last one is not a

\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}
-step. We denote by
\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{S}}
the reflexive-transitive
closure of
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}
, and similarly for
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}}
.
A reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
is

confluent
if for all
t,u_{1},u_{2}
such that
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u_{1}
and

t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u_{2}
, there is
s
such that
u_{1}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}s
and

u_{2}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}s
.

Theorem 1
.

\downarrow
The reductions
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}}
are
confluent.

A term
t
is a
surface
(resp.
full
)

normal form
if there is no
u
such that
t\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}u

(resp.
t\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}}u
). A term
t
is
surface
(resp.
full
)
normalizing
if
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{S}}u
(resp.
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{F}}u
) for some
surface (resp. full) normal form
u
. Since
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}\subsetneq\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}}
, some terms may be surface-normalizing
but not full-normalizing,
e.g.

\lambda x.\oc({\tt der}\!\left(\oc\Omega\right))
.

As a matter of fact, some ill-formed terms are not redexes but neither
represent a desired computation result. They are called
clashes

and have one of the following forms:

\mathtt{L}\!\left<\oc s\right>u\qquad s[x\backslash\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.u\right>]\qquad{\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.u\right>\right)\qquad t(\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.u\right>)\text{ if }t\neq\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\!\left<\lambda y.s\right>

This
static
notion of clash is lifted to a
dynamic

level. A term
t
is a
surface
(resp.
full
)

clash-free
if it does not surface (resp. full) reduce
to a term with a clash in surface (resp. full) position,
i.e.
if there are no surface (resp. full) context
\mathtt{S}

(resp.
\mathtt{F}
) and clash
c
such that
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{S}}\mathtt{S}\!\left<c\right>
(resp.
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{F}}\mathtt{F}\!\left<c\right>
). For example,

x\oc(y(\lambda z.z))
is surface clash-free but
not full clash-free as it has a clash
y(\lambda z.z)
under a
bang. Both notions
are stable under reduction
.

Finally, some terms contain neither redexes nor clashes. A

surface
(resp.
full
)
clash-free normal
form
is a surface (resp. full) normal form which is also
surface (resp. full) clash-free, as
e.g.
the term
xx
.
These are the desired results of the computation, and they can even be

syntactically
characterized by a tree grammar.

\displaystyle\mathtt{ne}_{\mathrm{S}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq x\in\mathcal{X}\;|\;\mathtt{ne}_{\mathrm{S}}\mathtt{na}_{\mathrm{S}}\;|\;{\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{ne}_{\mathrm{S}}\right)\;|\;\mathtt{ne}_{\mathrm{S}}[x\backslash\mathtt{ne}_{\mathrm{S}}]

\displaystyle\mathtt{na}_{\mathrm{S}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\oc t\;|\;\mathtt{ne}_{\mathrm{S}}\;|\;\mathtt{na}_{\mathrm{S}}[x\backslash\mathtt{ne}_{\mathrm{S}}]

\displaystyle\mathtt{nb}_{\mathrm{S}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathtt{ne}_{\mathrm{S}}\;|\;\lambda x.\mathtt{no}_{\mathrm{S}}\;|\;\mathtt{nb}_{\mathrm{S}}[x\backslash\mathtt{ne}_{\mathrm{S}}]

\displaystyle\mathtt{no}_{\mathrm{S}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathtt{na}_{\mathrm{S}}\;|\;\mathtt{nb}_{\mathrm{S}}

Lemma 2

(
[
20
]
)
.

Let
t\in\Lambda_{\oc}
, then
t\in\mathtt{no}_{\mathrm{S}}
iff
t
is a
surface clash-free normal form.

2.2
Quantitative Typing System

We present the quantitative typing system

\mathcal{B}

[
20
]
, based
on
[
40
,
33
]
. It contains functional and
intersection types. Here, intersections
are associative, commutative but
not idempotent
, thus an
intersection type is represented by a (possibly empty)
finite

multiset

\left[\sigma_{i}\right]_{i\in I}
. Formally, given a
countably infinite set
\mathcal{TV}
of type variables
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\dots
, we define by mutual induction:

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}(\textbf{Types})&\sigma,\tau,\rho&\coloneqq&\alpha\in\mathcal{TV}\;|\;\mathcal{M}\;|\;\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma\\
(\textbf{Multitypes})&\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}&\coloneqq&\left[\sigma_{i}\right]_{i\in I}\text{ where $I$ is a finite set}\end{array}

A (
type
)
environment
, noted
\Gamma
or

\Delta
, is a function from variables to multitypes, assigning the

empty multitype

\left[\,\right]
to all variables except a finite
number (possibly zero). The
empty environment
, noted

\emptyset
, maps every variable to
\left[\,\right]
. The
domain
of

\Gamma
is
{\tt dom}(\Gamma)=\{x\in\mathcal{X}\mid\Gamma(x)\neq\left[\,\right]\}
, the
image
of
\Gamma
is

{\tt im}(\Gamma)=\{\Gamma(x)\mid x\in{\tt dom}(\Gamma)\}
.
Given the environments
\Gamma
and
\Delta
,
\Gamma+\Delta
is the
environment mapping
x
to
\Gamma(x)\uplus\Delta(x)
, where

\uplus
denotes multiset union; and
+_{i\in I}\Delta_{i}
(with
I

finite) is its obvious extension to the non-binary case, in particular

+_{i\in I}\Delta_{i}=\emptyset
if
I=\emptyset
. An environment

\Gamma
is denoted by
x_{1}\!:\!\mathcal{M}_{1},\dots,x_{n}\!:\!\mathcal{M}_{n}
when the
x_{i}
’s are pairwise
distinct variables and

\Gamma(x_{i})=\mathcal{M}_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
,
and
\Gamma(y)=\left[\,\right]
for
y\notin\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
.

A
typing
is a pair
(\Gamma;\sigma)
, where

\Gamma
is an environment and
\sigma
is a type. A
(
typing
)
judgment
is a tuple of the form
\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
, where
(\Gamma;\sigma)
is a typing and

t
is a term (the
subject
of the judgment).
The typing system
\mathcal{B}
for
dBang
is defined
by the rules in
Figure

1
. The axiom
rule
(var)
is relevant,
i.e.
there is no weakening.
Rules
(abs)
,
(app)
and

(es)
are standard. Rule
(bg)
has as many
premises as elements in the finite (possibly empty) index set
I
,
the conclusion types
\oc u
with a multitype

gathering
all the (possibly different) types in the premises
typing
u
. In particular, when
I=\emptyset
, the rule has no
premises, and it types
any
term
\oc u
with

\left[\,\right]
, leaving the
subterm

u

untyped
. Rule

(der)
forces the argument of a dereliction to be typed
by a multitype of cardinality 1.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\\[-2.84544pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 29.76262pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(var)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$x:\left[\sigma\right]\vdash x:\sigma\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0375pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(app)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash t:\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}$}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma+\Delta\vdash t\,u:\sigma$}}\par}\par}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 9.18102pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(bg)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$(\Gamma_{i}\vdash t:\sigma_{i})_{i\in I}$}}\hbox{$+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i}\vdash\oc t:\left[\sigma_{i}\right]_{i\in I}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[16.0pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 2.36113pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(abs)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma,x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t:\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma$}}\par}\par}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 13.5618pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(es)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma,x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma$}}\hbox{$\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}$}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma+\Delta\vdash t[x\backslash u]:\sigma$}}\par}\par}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 6.76387pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(der)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash t:\left[\sigma\right]$}}\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash{\tt der}\!\left(t\right):\sigma$}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}

Figure 1:
Type System
\mathcal{B}
for the
dBang
-calculus.

A (
type
)
derivation
in system
\mathcal{B}

is a tree obtained by applying the rules in

Figure

1
. The judgment at the root of
the type derivation is the
conclusion
of the derivation. A
term
t
is
\mathcal{B}
-typable
if there is
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
for some typing

(\Gamma;\sigma)
, meaning that

\Pi
is a derivation in system
\mathcal{B}
with conclusion
\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
. We write
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\;\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
if there exists
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
.

System
\mathcal{B}
characterizes surface-normalizing
clash-free terms.

Theorem 3

(
[
20
,
12
]
)
.

Let
t,u\in\Lambda_{\oc}
.

1.

If
t\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}}u
, then for any typing

(\Gamma;\sigma)
, one has
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\;\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
if and only if
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\;\Gamma\vdash u:\sigma
.

2.

t
is
\mathcal{B}
-typable if and only if
t

surface-reduces to a surface clash-free normal form.

3
Meaningfulness = Typability + Inhabitation

In this section we introduce the notion of meaningfulness for

dBang
and we establish a logical characterization of
meaningful terms via system
\mathcal{B}
. Intuitively, a term

t
is meaningful if it can be supplied by some arguments (possibly
binding some free variables of
t
) so that it reduces to some
observable term. In
dBang
, the observables are the bang
terms since they are the only terms enabling substitution to be fired.

Definition 4
.

A term
t
is
dBang
-
meaningful
if there are a testing context
\mathtt{T}

and
u\in\Lambda_{\oc}
such that
\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{S}}\oc u
, where testing contexts are defined by the
grammar
\mathtt{T}\;\coloneqq\;\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{T}s\;|\;(\lambda x.\mathtt{T})s
.

For example,
\mathtt{I}
is
dBang
-meaningful using the testing
context
\mathtt{T}=\diamond\,\oc\oc u
. Both
\Omega
and

x\Omega
are
dBang
-meaningless: every testing
context they are plugged in cannot erase
\Omega
, which is not
normalizing. Note that the hole in a testing context is always in the
functional position of an application, in particular if the hole is in
the scope of some
\lambda
, then this
\lambda
must be applied.

(app)

(var)

(var)

x:\left[\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma\right]\vdash x:\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma

x:\left[\mathcal{M}\right]\vdash x:\mathcal{M}

x:\left[\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma,\mathcal{M}\right]\vdash xx:\sigma

Figure 2:
A type derivation of
xx
in
system
\mathcal{B}
.

(abs)

(bg)

(var)

x:\left[\alpha\right]\vdash x:\alpha

x:\left[\alpha\right]\vdash\oc x:\left[\alpha\right]

\emptyset\vdash\lambda x.\oc x:\left[\alpha\right]\Rightarrow\left[\alpha\right]

Figure 3:
Inhabitation of
\left[\alpha\right]\Rightarrow\left[\alpha\right]
in system
\mathcal{B}
.

In an adequate calculus, meaningfulness is usually characterized both
operationally (normalizability) and logically (typability): a term is
meaningful iff it is normalizing for a suitable subreduction of the
calculus iff it is typable in a suitable type system. Surprisingly,
these characterizations are subtler in
dBang
, because the
language has two (incompatible) data structures: abstractions (playing
the role of functions) and bangs (playing as values).

We are not aware of any operational characterization of

dBang
-meaningfulness; a natural candidate would be
normalizability by surface reduction, but this fails, even if we force
the obtained surface normal form to be clash-free. For instance, the
term
t_{0}\coloneqq xx
is
dBang
-meaningless
despite being a surface clash-free normal form. Indeed, for

xx
to be
dBang
-meaningful, a testing context

\mathtt{T}
would need to provide a term
u
to substitute the
variable
x
, so that
\mathtt{T}\!\left<xx\right>
would eventually reduce
to a bang. However, achieving this requires the mentioned term
u
to
reduce to both an abstraction and a bang, which is impossible. Hence,

dBang
-meaningfulness is not only the ability to produce a
surface clash-free normal form but also to transform this result into
an observable.

Concerning a logical characterization of

dBang
-meaningfulness, typability is not enough, at least
in system
\mathcal{B}
. For instance, the

dBang
-meaningless term
t_{0}\coloneqq xx
seen
above is typable in system
\mathcal{B}
. Every
type derivation of
t_{0}
has the form in

Figure

3
, which reveals the
conflict when assigning to
x
both an arrow type
\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma
(the type of terms eventually reducing to
abstractions) and a multitype
\mathcal{M}
(the type of terms
eventually reducing to bangs). The inhabitation problem can
be used to detect such conflicts, allowing for a handy
characterization of meaningfulness. Indeed, the multitype
\left[\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma,\mathcal{M}\right]
assigned to the variable
x
in

Figure

3
is not inhabited.

While it is complex to syntactically establish operational conditions
such as (not) reducing to abstractions or bangs, this is easily
achieved semantically. Indeed, we establish a logical characterization
of
dBang
-meaningfulness based on
typability
and

inhabitation
in system
\mathcal{B}
, similarly to what
happens in the
\lambda
-calculus with
pairs
[
11
,
26
,
23
]
.
Intuitively, suppose that a term
t
is
dBang
-meaningful,
so there is a testing context
\mathtt{T}
such that
\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>

reduces to an observable,
i.e.
a bang, which can be typed with the
typing
(\emptyset;\left[\,\right])
in system
\mathcal{B}
.
By
Theorem

3
.
1
,

\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>
must also be typable by the same typing

(\emptyset;\left[\,\right])
, meaning that
t
is
typable
by an
environment
x_{1}\!:\!\mathcal{M}_{1},\dots,x_{n}\!:\!\mathcal{M}_{m}
and a type
\mathcal{N}_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{n}\Rightarrow\left[\,\right]
, where all the
\mathcal{M}_{i}
’s and

\mathcal{N}_{i}
’s are
inhabited
.

A similar argument holds for other type systems and
calculi
[
23
]
with their own notions of
meaningfulness and observable. The point is to identify the set of
types
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\tt obs}
associated with the observables.
Given any type system
\mathcal{S}
, whose types are those of

Section

2.2
, and a set of types

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\tt obs}
for observable terms, the set of

arguments

{\tt args}_{\mathcal{S}}(\sigma)
of a type

\sigma
is the set of multitypes appearing to the left of arrows,
until reaching the type of an observable. Formally, if
\sigma\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\tt obs}
then
{\tt args}_{\mathcal{S}}(\sigma)\coloneqq\emptyset
,
otherwise

{\tt args}_{\mathcal{S}}(\alpha)\coloneqq\emptyset
,

{\tt args}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma)\coloneqq\{\mathcal{M}\}\cup{\tt args}_{\mathcal{S}}(\sigma)
, and

{\tt args}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{M})=\emptyset
. For example, in system

\mathcal{B}
where
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\tt obs}
contains all
multitypes,
{\tt args}_{\mathcal{B}}([\tau]\Rightarrow(\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\left[\alpha\right]))=\{\left[\tau\right],\mathcal{M}\}
. The

dCBN
and

dCBN
cases are discussed in
Section

5
.

Definition 5
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a type system and
{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{S}}\!\left(\cdot\right)

be a predicate on the types of
\mathcal{S}
. A set
S
of
types is
inhabited
, noted
{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{S}}\!\left(S\right)
, if

{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{S}}\!\left(\sigma\right)
for all
\sigma\in S
. We write

{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{S}}\!\left(\Gamma\right)
if

{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{S}}\!\left({\tt im}(\Gamma)\right)
. A typing

(\Gamma;\sigma)
(resp. judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
) is
\mathcal{S}
-
testable
if

{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{S}}\!\left(\Gamma\right)
and

{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{S}}\!\left({\tt args}_{\mathcal{S}}\!\left(\sigma\right)\right)
. A term
t
is

\mathcal{S}
-testable
if
\triangleright_{\mathcal{S}}\;\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
for some
\mathcal{S}
-testable typing

(\Gamma;\sigma)
.

A type
\sigma
is
inhabited
in system
\mathcal{B}
,
noted
{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{B}}\!\left(\sigma\right)
, if
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\emptyset\vdash t:\sigma
for some
\Pi
and
t
. For instance, in system

\mathcal{B}
, the type
\left[\,\right]
is inhabited using rule
(bg) with empty premises, and the
environment
\emptyset
is trivially
inhabited. The type
\left[\alpha\right]\Rightarrow\left[\alpha\right]
is also
inhabited, see
Figure

3
.

Lemma 6
.

\downarrow
Let
t\in\Lambda_{\oc}
and
\mathtt{T}
be a testing context.
If
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\;\emptyset\vdash\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>:\left[\,\right]
,
then
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
with

{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{B}}\!\left(\Gamma\right)
and

{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{B}}\!\left({\tt args}_{\mathcal{B}}\!\left(\sigma\right)\right)
.

Inhabitation serves as a crucial tool to produce an observable from a
typable term. As discussed previously, any type of a variable
x
from
the environment of a meaningful term
t
should be inhabited. Hence,
we need the type environment
\Gamma
to be inhabited. However,
relying solely on the inhabitation of
\Gamma
is not sufficient, as
illustrated by the typable term
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\;\emptyset\vdash\lambda x.xx:\left[\left[\mathcal{M}\right]\Rightarrow\tau,\mathcal{M}\right]\Rightarrow\tau
, which, despite having a trivially inhabited environment, is

dBang
-meaningless. We thus also test the inhabitation of type arguments of
the given type
\sigma
. This therefore means that

\mathcal{B}
-testability is sufficient to ensure
dBang
-meaningfulness.
Surprisingly, this actually provides a characterization of

dBang
-meaningfulness.

Theorem 7

(Logical Characterization)
.

\downarrow
Let
t\in\Lambda_{\oc}
. Then
t
is

dBang
-meaningful if and only if
\,\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
for some

\mathcal{B}
-testable typing
(\Gamma;\sigma)
.

A
\bm{\lambda}_{\texttt{dBang}}
-theory
is an equivalence
\equiv
on

\Lambda_{\oc}
containing
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}}
and closed under full
contexts. Let
\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}
(also noted
\equiv_{\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}}
) be
the smallest
\bm{\lambda}_{\texttt{dBang}}
-theory equating all

dBang
-meaningless terms.
Theorem

7

entails that
\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}
is
consistent
, that is, it does not
equate all terms.

Proposition 8

(Consistency of
\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}
)
.

\downarrow
There exist
t,u\in\Lambda_{\oc}
such that
t\not\equiv_{\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}}u
.

We also corroborate our definition of meaningfulness by proving that
it fulfills a genericity property and showing that
\mathcal{H}

admits a unique maximal consistent extension
(
Section

4
) and subsumes the well-established
notions of meaningfulness for
dCBN
and
dCBV
(
Section

5
).

4
Typed and Surface Genericity in
dBang

In
Section

3
, we proved that

dBang
-meaningfulness is captured by typability in system

\mathcal{B}
with some
\mathcal{B}
-testable typing.
While this concise characterization as “meaningfulness = typability +
inhabitation”
[
23
]
provides a high level
understanding, its practical manipulation might pose some challenges.
Suppose we study some properties of a
dBang
-meaningful
term
t
through the logical characterization
(
Theorem

7
), thus having
a type derivation
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
with

(\Gamma;\sigma)

\mathcal{B}
-testable. If we proceed
by induction on
\Pi
, then there is no guarantee that all the
judgments appearing in
\Pi
have
\mathcal{B}
-testable typings
as well, which would make the reasoning awkward and the logical
characterization of

Theorem

7
difficult to
exploit. But this is not the case. Upcoming

Lemma

9
states that

\mathcal{B}
-testability propagates bottom-up: if the conclusion
of a derivation
\Pi
has a
\mathcal{B}
-testable typing, then
so does every other judgment in
\Pi
.

We write
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}_{\tt m}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
if
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
and each judgment in

\Pi
is

\mathcal{B}
-testable, and
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}_{\tt m}}t
if

\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}_{\tt m}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
for some typing

(\Gamma;\sigma)
.

Lemma 9
.

\downarrow
Let
t\in\Lambda_{\oc}
:
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
with

(\Gamma;\sigma)

\mathcal{B}
-testable iff
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}_{\tt m}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
.

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
): Trivial.
(
\Rightarrow
): By an induction on
\Pi
.
∎

We can therefore easily reason using the
logical characterization of meaningfulness, resulting in the following first genericity result for

dBang
.

Theorem 10

(Typed Genericity)
.

\downarrow
Let
t\in\Lambda_{\oc}
be
dBang
-meaningless and

\mathtt{F}
be a full context. If
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}_{\tt m}}\;\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>:\sigma
, then
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}_{\tt m}}\;\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>:\sigma
for all
u\in\Lambda_{\oc}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\mathtt{F}
, using both

Theorems

7
and
9
.
∎

This proof relies entirely on the fact that the meaningless subterm

t
cannot be explicitly typed in any of the judgments of
\Pi
, as
typing
t
in
\mathcal{B}_{\tt m}
is equivalent to being

dBang
-meaningful (by

Theorems

7
and
9
).
Thus, the typed genericity theorem fails when weakening the hypothesis
from
\mathcal{B}_{\tt m}
-typability to

\mathcal{B}
-typability. Consider for example the

dBang
-meaningless term
t=xx
and the context

\mathtt{F}=y\,\diamond
, then
\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>
is

\mathcal{B}
-typable as witnessed by
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\;y:\left[\mathcal{N}\Rightarrow\alpha\right],x:\left[\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}\right]\vdash\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>:\alpha
–note that the type of
x
is not inhabited–
while
\mathtt{F}\!\left<\Omega\right>
is not
\mathcal{B}
-typable.

As a consequence, we can prove a qualitative
(surface) genericity result. We call it
surface
,
despite it universally quantify over full contexts, as

dBang
-meaningful is defined in terms of
surface reduction. The corresponding results for
dCBN
and

dCBV
are also called
surface
in
[
14
]
and
light
in
[
9
]
, later generalized to a
stratified

notion in
[
14
]
.

Corollary 11

(Qualitative Surface Genericity)
.

Let
\mathtt{F}
be a full context. If
\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>
is

dBang
-meaningful for some
dBang
-meaningless
t\in\Lambda_{\oc}
, then
\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>
is

dBang
-meaningful
for all
u\in\Lambda_{\oc}
.

Proof.

Let
u\!\in\!\Lambda_{\oc}
. As
\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>
is

dBang
-meaningful, then there is
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}_{\tt m}}\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>
by
Theorem

7

and
Lemma

9
. As
t
is

dBang
-meaningless, then there is
\Pi^{\prime}\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}_{\tt m}}\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>
by
Theorem

10
, and hence

\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>
is
dBang
-meaningful
by
Theorem

7

and
Lemma

9
.
∎

Genericity is a sanity check on meaningfulness: it holds only if all

dBang
-meaningless terms are
truly
meaningless.
Still, some truly meaningless terms might be misinterpreted as

dBang
-meaningful. Indeed, when crafting a notion of

dBang
-meaningless that would satisfy genericity, one might
not take
all
truly meaningless terms.
The
\bm{\lambda}_{\texttt{dBang}}
-theory
\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}^{*}
is introduced to avoid that.
Let
\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}^{*}
, also noted
\equiv_{\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}^{*}}
, be the relation on
\Lambda_{\oc}
defined by:

\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}^{*}\coloneqq\{(t,u)\;|\;\forall\;\mathtt{F}\text{ full context},\,\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>\text{{dBang}-meaningful}\Leftrightarrow\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>\text{{dBang}-meaningful}\}

We expect
\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}^{*}
to be an extension of the theory

\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}
. Moreover, to check that all truly meaningless terms
are actually
dBang
-meaningless, we also want this theory
to
maximal
, meaning that no term can be additional equated
without compromising consistency.

Theorem 12
.

\downarrow
\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}^{*}
is the unique maximal consistent
\bm{\lambda}_{\texttt{dBang}}
-theory
containing
\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{\texttt{dBang}}
.

5
Subsuming CBN and CBV Meaningfulness

In this section we show that the notions of meaningfulness for

dCBN
and
dCBV
in the
literature
[
14
]
are subsumed by the one
proposed in
Section

3
for
dBang
. We also
deduce surface genericity for
dCBN
and
dCBV
as
a consequence of surface genericity for
dBang
.

5.1
dCBN
and
dCBV
Calculi

Both
dCBN

[
6
,
7
,
1
]
and

dCBV

[
10
]
are specified using ES and
action at a distance, as explained in
Section

2.1
for

dBang
, and they share the same term syntax. The sets

\Lambda
of
terms
and
\Upsilon
of
values

are inductively defined below.

\textbf{(Terms)}\ \ t,u\;\Coloneqq\;v\;|\;t\,u\;|\;t[x\backslash u]\hskip 28.45274pt\textbf{(Values)}\ \ v\;\Coloneqq\;x\;|\;\lambda x.t

Note that the syntax contains neither
{\tt der}
nor
\oc
. The
distinction between terms and values is irrelevant in
dCBN
but crucial in
dCBV
. The two calculi also share the same

list contexts

\mathtt{L}
, but use specialized

surface contexts

\mathtt{N}
and
\mathtt{V}

respectively. Again, contexts can be seen as terms with exactly one

hole

\diamond
and are inductively defined below.

\begin{array}[]{r rcl}\textbf{(List Contexts)}&\mathtt{L}&\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{L}[x\backslash t]\\[-2.0pt]
\textbf{({dCBN} Surface Contexts)}&\mathtt{N}&\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{N}\,t\;|\;\lambda x.\mathtt{N}\;|\;\mathtt{N}[x\backslash t]\\[-2.0pt]
\textbf{({dCBV} Surface Contexts)}&\mathtt{V}&\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{V}\,t\;|\;t\,\mathtt{V}\;|\;\mathtt{V}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash\mathtt{V}]\\[-2.0pt]
\textbf{(Full Contexts)}&\mathtt{F}&\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{F}\,t\;|\;t\,\mathtt{F}\;|\;\lambda x.\mathtt{F}\;|\;\mathtt{F}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash\mathtt{F}]\end{array}

The differences between
dCBN
and
dCBV
are in
the previous notions of
surface
contexts, as well as the
following
rewrite rules
.

\displaystyle\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>u

\displaystyle\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}\mathtt{L}\!\left<t[x\backslash u]\right>

\displaystyle t[x\backslash u]

\displaystyle\mapsto_{{\tt s}}t\{x\backslash u\}

\displaystyle t[x\backslash\mathtt{L}\!\left<v\right>]

\displaystyle\mapsto_{{\tt sV}}\mathtt{L}\!\left<t\{x\backslash v\}\right>

Rules
dB
and
sV
are
both capture-free: no free variable of
u
(resp.
t
) is captured by
the list context
\mathtt{L}
.
The
dCBN
surface reduction

\rightarrow_{\mathrm{N}}
is
defined as the union of the
dCBN
surface closure of
rewrite rules
dB
and
s
, while the

dCBV
surface reduction

\rightarrow_{\mathrm{V}}
is the union
of the
dCBV
surface closure of the rewrite rules

dB
and
sV
.
Finally, we use
\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{N}}
(resp.
\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{V}}
) to denote the reflexive transitive closure of the
relation
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{N}}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{V}}
).

Example 13
.

For example,
t_{0}\coloneqq(\lambda x.yxx)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})\rightarrow_{\mathrm{N}}(yxx)[x\backslash\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{N}}y(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})\eqqcolon t_{1}
and
t_{0}=(\lambda x.yxx)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})\rightarrow_{\mathrm{V}}(yxx)[x\backslash\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{V}}(yxx)[x\backslash z[z\backslash\mathtt{I}]]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{V}}(yxx)[x\backslash\mathtt{I}]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{V}}y\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}\eqqcolon t_{2}
.

Note that the
dCBN
surface reduction is nothing but (a
non-deterministic diamond variant of) the well-known
head

reduction
[
18
]
.

The quantitative type systems
\mathcal{N}
for
dCBN
and
\mathcal{V}
for
dCBV
are presented in
Figures

4
and
5
, respectively, they were studied in

[
20
,
21
]
.

Types
and
judgments
are the same as for system

\mathcal{B}
. A derivation
\Pi
in system
\mathcal{N}
with
conclusion
\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
is noted
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
; we write
\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\;\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
if there is a
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
. We use similar notations
for system
\mathcal{V}
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\\[-2.84544pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 29.76262pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(var)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$x:\left[\sigma\right]\vdash x:\sigma\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(app)$ }}}\hbox{\hskip 1.6184pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash t:\left[\tau_{i}\right]_{i\in I}\Rightarrow\sigma\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$(\Delta_{i}\vdash u:\tau_{i})_{i\in I}$}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma+_{i\in I}\Delta_{i}\vdash t\,u:\sigma$}}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[16.0pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 2.36113pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(abs)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma,x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t:\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma$}}\par}\par}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 7.9059pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(es)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma,x:\left[\tau_{i}\right]_{i\in I}\vdash t:\sigma$}}\hbox{$(\Delta_{i}\vdash u:\tau_{i})_{i\in I}$}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma+_{i\in I}\Delta_{i}\vdash t[x\backslash u]:\sigma$}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}

Figure 4:
Type System
\mathcal{N}
for the
dCBN
-calculus.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\\[-2.84544pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 29.04854pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(var)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$x:\mathcal{M}\vdash x:\mathcal{M}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0375pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(app)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash t:\left[\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma\right]\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}$}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma+\Delta\vdash t\,u:\sigma$}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[16.0pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 10.15326pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(abs)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$(\Gamma_{i},x:\mathcal{M}_{i}\vdash t:\sigma_{i})_{i\in I}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i}\vdash\lambda x.t:\left[\mathcal{M}_{i}\Rightarrow\sigma_{i}\right]_{i\in I}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 13.5618pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$(es)$ }}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma,x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma$}}\hbox{$\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}$}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma+\Delta\vdash t[x\backslash u]:\sigma$}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}

Figure 5:
Type System
\mathcal{V}
for the
dCBV
-calculus.

The salient property of type systems
\mathcal{N}
and

\mathcal{V}
is characterizing normalization in
dCBN
and
dCBV
, respectively.

Lemma 14

(
[
20
,
21
]
)
.

Let
t\in\Lambda
, then:

t
is
dCBN
surface normalizing iff
it is
\mathcal{N}
-typable.

t
is
dCBV
surface normalizing iff
it is
\mathcal{V}
-typable.

Both
dCBN
and
dCBV
can be embedded into

dBang
by decorating each term with the
\oc
and

{\tt der}\!\left(\cdot\right)
modalities. The embedding for
dCBN
is
straightforward, while various embeddings for
dCBV
have
been proposed in the literature

[
41
,
53
,
54
,
43
,
20
,
21
,
13
]
, each with its own strengths and weaknesses. In this
work, we use the embeddings from

[
20
,
21
]
defined
below:

\begin{array}[]{rcl c rcl}{x}^{\tt n}&\coloneqq&x&&{x}^{\tt v}&\coloneqq&\oc x\\
{(\lambda x.t)}^{\tt n}&\coloneqq&\lambda x.{t}^{\tt n}&&{(\lambda x.t)}^{\tt v}&\coloneqq&\oc\lambda x.{t}^{\tt v}\\
{(tu)}^{\tt n}&\coloneqq&{t}^{\tt n}\,\oc{u}^{\tt n}&&{(tu)}^{\tt v}&\coloneqq&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathtt{L}\!\left<s\right>{u}^{\tt v}&\text{if}\;{t}^{\tt v}=\mathtt{L}\!\left<\oc s\right>\\
{\tt der}\!\left({t}^{\tt v}\right){u}^{\tt v}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
{(t[x\backslash u])}^{\tt n}&\coloneqq&{t}^{\tt n}[x\backslash\oc{u}^{\tt n}]&&{(t[x\backslash u])}^{\tt v}&\coloneqq&{t}^{\tt v}[x\backslash{u}^{\tt v}]\end{array}

These translations are extended to contexts as expected by setting

{\diamond}^{\tt n}\coloneqq\diamond
and
{\diamond}^{\tt v}\coloneqq\diamond
.

Example 15
.

Recalling
Example

13
, one has

{t}^{\tt n}_{0}=(\lambda x.y\,\oc x\,\oc x)\,\oc(\mathtt{I}\oc\mathtt{I})
,
{t}^{\tt n}_{1}=y\,\oc(\mathtt{I}\oc\mathtt{I})\,\oc(\mathtt{I}\oc\mathtt{I})
,

{t}^{\tt v}_{0}=(\lambda x.({\tt der}\!\left(y\,\oc x\right)\oc x))((\lambda z.\oc z)\,\oc(\lambda z.\oc z))
and
{t}^{\tt v}_{2}={\tt der}\!\left(y\,\oc(\lambda z.\oc z)\right)\oc(\lambda z.\oc z)
.

Let us give some intuition on these embeddings. In
dCBN
,
any argument (right-hand side of application or substitution) can be
erased/duplicated, as bang terms in the
dBang
-calculus, so
that arguments must be translated to bang terms. In
dCBV
,
only values can be erased/duplicated so that values must be translated
to bang terms. However, this remark alone is not sufficient to achieve
a
dCBV
embedding enjoying good properties, and in
particular to translate
dCBV
-normal forms into

dBang
-normal forms. The translation of applications is
precisely designed in order to guarantee this property.

These embeddings preserve reductions, which will allows us to show
that meaningfulness if preserved through embedding.

Lemma 16

(Simulation
[
20
,
21
]
)
.

Let
t,u\in\Lambda
.

1.

If
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{N}}u
then
{t}^{\tt n}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{S}}{u}^{\tt n}
.

2.

If
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{V}}u
then
{t}^{\tt v}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{S}}{u}^{\tt v}
.

Example 17
.

In
Example

13
, we showed that
t_{0}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{N}}t_{1}
and
t_{0}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{V}}t_{2}
. Recalling

Example

15
, one has
{t}^{\tt n}_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}(y\,\oc x\,\oc x)[x\backslash\oc(\mathtt{I}\oc\mathtt{I})]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}{t}^{\tt n}_{1}
and
{t}^{\tt v}_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}({\tt der}\!\left(y\,\oc x\right)\oc x)[x\backslash((\lambda z.\oc z)\,\oc(\lambda z.\oc z))]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}({\tt der}\!\left(y\,\oc x\right)\oc x)[x\backslash(\oc z)[z\backslash\oc(\lambda z.\oc z)]]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}({\tt der}\!\left(y\,\oc x\right)\oc x)[x\backslash\oc(\lambda z.\oc z)]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{S}}{t}^{\tt v}_{2}
.

As the
dCBV
-embedding uses
{\tt der}
,
some
{\tt d}\oc
-step might be needed in the simulation process.

These embeddings also preserve typing, which will make possible to
project
dBang
meaningfulness and genericity onto

dCBN
and
dCBV
. More precisely, the two
embeddings are proven to be sound and complete with respect to system

\mathcal{B}
.

Proposition 18

(
[
20
,
21
]
)
.

Let
t\in\Lambda
.

1.

One has
\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\;\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
if and only if
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\;\Gamma\vdash{t}^{\tt n}:\sigma
.

2.

One has
\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\;\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
if and only if
\triangleright_{\mathcal{B}}\;\Gamma\vdash{t}^{\tt v}:\sigma
.

A straightforward corollary is that
dCBN
and

dCBV
inhabitation properties are well subsumed in

dBang
, as illustrated in
[
12
]
.
In simpler words, any type inhabited in
dCBN
(resp.
dCBV
) is also inhabited in
dBang
. As expected,
the converse is false.

In
dCBV
and
dBang
, typing an arbitrary term
and typing an argument is similar, as it
can be seen in the right premise
\Delta\vdash u:\mathcal{M}
of the typing
rules (app) and (es) of systems

\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{B}
. This is not the case in

dCBN
, as the right premise of the (app)
and (es) rules of system
\mathcal{N}
requires,
not a
single
derivation, but a

set

(\Delta_{i}\vdash u:\tau_{i})_{i\in I}
of
typing derivation for the same term
u
. In the
logical
characterization (
Theorem

7
), we
check that arguments of a
given type can be inhabited. We therefore need to
reflect the typability of arguments
—rather than typablity of arbitrary terms— in the definition of
dCBN
inhabitation.

Definition 19
.

In system
\mathcal{N}
, a non-multitype
\sigma
is

inhabited
, noted
{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{N}}\!\left(\sigma\right)
, if
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\emptyset\vdash t:\sigma
for some
\Pi
and
t
. A
multitype
\left[\tau_{i}\right]_{i\in I}
is
inhabited
in system

\mathcal{N}
, noted
{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{N}}\!\left(\left[\tau_{i}\right]_{i\in I}\right)
if there exists
u\in\Lambda
such that for each
i\in I
,
\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\;\emptyset\vdash u:\tau_{i}
.

In system
\mathcal{V}
, a type
\sigma
is
inhabited
,
noted
{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{V}}\!\left(\sigma\right)
, if
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\emptyset\vdash t:\sigma
for some
\Pi
and
t
.

In particular, the type
\left[\,\right]
is inhabited
in both
dCBN
and
dCBV
(
i.e.

{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{N}}\!\left(\left[\,\right]\right)
and
{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{V}}\!\left(\left[\,\right]\right)
).
Similarly, the environment
\emptyset
is also trivially inhabited in both
(
i.e.

{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{N}}\!\left(\emptyset\right)
and
{\tt inh}_{\mathcal{V}}\!\left(\emptyset\right)
).

5.2
dCBN
Meaningfulness and Surface Genericity

In this subsection, our attention shifts towards the

dCBN
-calculus, where we show that its notion of
meaningfulness is subsumed by that of
dBang
.
This observation enables us to project the surface genericity theorem
accordingly.
We start by introducing
dCBN
-meaningfulness.

Definition 20
.

A term
t\in\Lambda
is
dCBN
-
meaningful

if there is a testing context
\mathtt{T}
such that
\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{N}}\mathtt{I}
, where testing contexts are defined by
\mathtt{T}\Coloneqq\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{T}\,u\;|\;(\lambda x.\mathtt{T})\,u
.
1
1

1

Usually,
dCBN
-meaningfulness (aka

solvability
) is defined using contexts of the form

(\lambda x_{1}\dots x_{m}.\diamond)N_{1}\dots N_{n}
(
m,n\geq 0
)

[
17
,
18
,
64
]
, instead of testing
contexts. It is easy to check that the two definitions are
equivalent in
dCBN
. The benefit of our definition is
that the same testing contexts are also used to define

dCBV
-meaningfulness (
Section

5.3
).

For example
t=x(\lambda y.\Omega)
is

dCBN
-meaningful as
\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{N}}\mathtt{I}
for

\mathtt{T}=(\lambda x.\diamond)(\lambda z.\mathtt{I})
, while
\Omega

and
\lambda x.\Omega
are
dCBN
-meaningless as for whatever
testing context
\Omega
and
\lambda x.\Omega
are plugged into,

\Omega
will not be erased. Finally, based on
this definition of meaningfulness it is quite natural to
defined the types of
observable terms in
dCBN
as the identity types (
i.e.

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{N}}^{\tt obs}\coloneqq\{[\sigma]\Rightarrow\sigma\;|\;\sigma\text{ type}\}
).

Unlike
dBang
,

dCBN
-meaningfulness can be characterized both

operationally
, through surface normalizability, and

logically
, through typability in system
\mathcal{N}
.
Moreover, this logical characterization
turns out to be equivalent to
\mathcal{N}
-testability
, meaning
that
dCBN
-meaningfulness can also be characterized via typability and inhabitation, as
already observed in
[
24
]
.

Lemma 21

(Characterizations of
dCBN
-Meaningfulness
[
26
,
24
,
20
]
)
.

\downarrow
Let
t\in\Lambda
.

1.

(Operational)

t
is
dCBN
-meaningful
iff
t
is
dCBN
surface-normalizing.

2.

(Logical)
(1)
t
is
dCBN
-meaningful iff
(2)
t
is

\mathcal{N}
-typable iff (3)
t
is

\mathcal{N}
-testable.

Thanks to the specific shape of
dCBN
-normal forms, we can
always type a
dCBN
-meaningful term
t
by a typing

(\Gamma;\sigma)
such that the
non-empty
multitypes in

\Gamma
and
{\tt args}_{\mathcal{N}}\!\left(\sigma\right)
are of the form
\left[\left[\,\right]\Rightarrow\cdots\left[\,\right]\Rightarrow\left[\alpha\right]\Rightarrow\alpha\right]
. These types are trivially inhabited by erasers of the form

\lambda x_{1}.\cdots\lambda x_{n}.\mathtt{I}
, used in the proof that

\mathcal{N}
-typability implies
dCBN
-meaningfulness.

Having an operational characterization of meaningfulness seems to
point out that transforming a result into something observable is a
trivial operation in
dCBN
. Indeed, using simulation
(
Lemma

16
.(
2
)),
we easily show that
dCBN
-meaningful is preserved by the

dCBN
-embedding, thus confirming this intuition. Moreover,
and thanks to the logical characterization
(
Lemma

21
.(
2
)),
we show that the converse also holds, yielding the following result.

Theorem 22
.

Let
t\in\Lambda
, then
t
is
dCBN
-meaningful
iff
{t}^{\tt n}
is
dBang
-meaningful.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)

We present here an operational proof.
Let
t
be
dCBN
-meaningful, thus

\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{N}}\mathtt{I}
for some testing context
\mathtt{T}
.
By induction on
\mathtt{T}
, one has that

{(\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>)}^{\tt n}={\mathtt{T}}^{\tt n}\!\left<{t}^{\tt n}\right>
. By
simulation
(
Lemma

16
.
1
),
one deduces that

{\mathtt{T}}^{\tt n}\!\left<{t}^{\tt n}\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{S}}\lambda x.x
thus

{\mathtt{T}}^{\tt n}\!\left<{t}^{\tt n}\right>\oc\oc y\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{S}}(\lambda x.x)\oc\oc y\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{S}}\oc y
.
Notice that
{\mathtt{T}}^{\tt n}\oc\oc y
is a

dBang
-testing context. We thus conclude that

{t}^{\tt n}
is
dBang
-meaningful.

(\Leftarrow)

Let
{t}^{\tt n}
be

dBang
-meaningful, then using

Theorem

7
, it is

\mathcal{B}
-testable and thus
\mathcal{B}
-typable.
By

Proposition

18
.
1
,

t
is
\mathcal{N}
-typable and hence
t
is

dCBN
-meaningful by

Lemma

21
.
∎

Theorem

22
states that

dCBN
-meaningfulness precisely aligns with

dBang
-meaningfulness on its image, strengthening the idea
that these two notions are adequately chosen.
Thanks to
Theorem

22
, we can now
project surface genericity from
dBang
to
dCBN
.

Theorem 23

(
dCBN
Qualitative Surface Genericity)
.

Let
\mathtt{F}
be a full context. If
\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>
is

dCBN
-meaningful for some

dCBN
-meaningless
t\in\Lambda
, then

\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>
is
dCBN
-meaningful
for
every
u\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

Let
t\in\Lambda
be
dCBN
-meaningless and

\mathtt{F}
be a full context. Suppose that
\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>
is

dCBN
-meaningful, then using

Theorem

22
,
and the fact that

{(\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>)}^{\tt n}={\mathtt{F}}^{\tt n}\!\left<{t}^{\tt n}\right>
(simple
induction on
\mathtt{F}
), is

dBang
-meaningful, and
{t}^{\tt n}
is

dBang
-meaningless. By

Corollary

11
, for any
u\in\Lambda
,

{\mathtt{F}}^{\tt n}\!\left<{u}^{\tt n}\right>={(\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>)}^{\tt n}
is
dBang
-meaningful, and hence
\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>
is

dCBN
-meaningful using

Theorem

22
.
∎

5.3
dCBV
Meaningfulness and Surface Genericity

We now move to the
dCBV
-calculus, where we show that its
notion of meaningfulness is subsumed by that of the

dBang
-calculus, and then project surface genericity
theorem accordingly.

Adapting meaningfulness from
dCBN
to
dCBV
by
replacing
dCBN
-reduction with
dCBV
-reduction
may seem initially promising. This notion, known as

dCBV
-solvability, has appealing
properties
[
60
,
64
,
10
,
27
,
44
,
5
]
.
Unfortunately, Accattoli and Guerrieri showed that genericity fails in
such setting
[
5
]
, and that equating
unsolvable terms yields an inconsistent
theory
[
5
]
. Consequently,

dCBV
-meaningfulness cannot be identified with

dCBV
-solvability. Identifying appropriate notions to
capture
dCBV
meaningful
\lambda
-terms and formally
validating these notions has been a longstanding and
challenging open question.

Paolini and Ronchi Della Rocca
[
60
,
64
]
introduced
the notion of
potentially valuability
for

CBV
, also studied in

[
59
,
10
,
27
,
39
]

and renamed
(
dCBV
) scrutability
in

[
5
]
. This notion, which we introduce below,
proves to be suitable
dCBV
-meaningfulness. Notably, it
aligns seamlessly with
dBang
-meaningfulness through the

dCBV
-embedding and thus enjoys a genericity theorem.

Definition 24
.

A term
t\in\Lambda
is

dCBV
-
meaningful
if there exists a testing
context
\mathtt{T}
and a value
v
such that

\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{V}}v
, where testing contexts are defined by

\mathtt{T}\;\Coloneqq\;\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{T}\,u\;|\;(\lambda x.\mathtt{T})\,u
.

For example
t=x(\lambda y.z)
is
dCBV
-meaningful
as
\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{V}}\lambda y.z
for
\mathtt{T}=(\lambda x.\diamond)(\lambda z.z)
, while
\Omega
and

x\Omega
are
dCBV
-meaningless as for whatever
testing context
\Omega
and
x\Omega
are plugged into,

\Omega
will not be erased.

Notice that this definition closely mirrors that of

dBang
-meaningfulness, with the primary difference being the
replacement of
dBang
values
for those of
dCBV
. Since
values are typed with multitypes, it is natural to take them as types
of the observable terms in
dCBV
(
i.e.

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\tt obs}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{M}\;|\;\mathcal{M}\text{ multitype}\}
). Consequently, and thanks to the
preservation of typing
(
Proposition

18
.
2
),
one easily shows that testability is preserved through the

dCBV
translation: if a term
t
is

\mathcal{V}
-testable, then its image
{t}^{\tt v}
is

\mathcal{B}
-testable.

As in
dCBN
and unlike

dBang
,
dCBV
-meaningfulness can
actually be characterized both
operationally
, through surface
normalizability, and
logically
, through typability in
system
\mathcal{V}
. Moreover, the
logical characterization turns out to be equivalent to

\mathcal{V}
-testability, meaning that

dCBV
-meaningfulness is also characterized by means of
typability and inhabitation.

Lemma 25

(Characterizations of
dCBV
-Meaningfulness
[
10
,
5
,
20
]
)
.

\downarrow
Let
t\in\Lambda
.

1.

(Operational)

t
is
dCBV
-meaningful iff
t
is
dCBV
surface-normalizing.

2.

(Logical)
(1)
t
is
dCBV
-meaningful iff (2)
t
is

\mathcal{V}
-typable iff (3)
t
is

\mathcal{V}
-testable.

The notion of observable aligns in
dCBV
and

dBang
, at least from the type perspective. This yields a
simple fully semantical proof of the preservation of

dCBV
-meaningfulness.

Theorem 26
.

Let
t\in\Lambda
, then
t
is
dCBV
-meaningful
iff
{t}^{\tt v}
is
dBang
-meaningful.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)

We present here a semantical proof. Let
t
be

dCBV
-meaningful, then using

Lemma

25
, one has that
t

is
\mathcal{V}
-testable thus, by preservation of
testability,
{t}^{\tt v}
is
\mathcal{B}
-testable
and one concludes that
{t}^{\tt v}
is

dBang
-meaningful according to

Theorem

7
.

(\Leftarrow)

Let
{t}^{\tt v}
be

dBang
-meaningful, then using

Theorem

7
, it is

\mathcal{B}
-testable thus
\mathcal{B}
-typable.
By

Proposition

18
.
2
,

t
is
\mathcal{V}
-typable and thus
t
is

dCBV
-meaningful by

Lemma

25
.
∎

Theorem

26
states that

dCBV
-meaningfulness precisely aligns with

dBang
-meaningfulness on its image, strengthening the idea
that these two notions are adequately chosen. Thanks to

Theorem

26
, we can now project
surface genericity from
dBang
to
dCBV
.

Theorem 27

(
dCBV
Qualitative Surface Genericity)
.

Let
\mathtt{F}
be a full context. If
\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>
is

dCBV
-meaningful for some

dCBV
-meaningless
t\in\Lambda
, then

\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>
is
dCBV
-meaningful
for
every
u\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

Let
t\in\Lambda
be
dCBV
-meaningless and

\mathtt{F}
be a full context. Suppose that
\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>
is

dCBV
-meaningful, then using

Theorem

26
,

{(\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>)}^{\tt v}
is
dBang
-meaningful, and

{t}^{\tt v}
is
dBang
-meaningless. By induction
on
\mathtt{F}
,
{(\mathtt{F}\!\left<t\right>)}^{\tt v}={\mathtt{F}}^{\tt v}\!\left<{t}^{\tt v}\right>
thus

{\mathtt{F}}^{\tt v}\!\left<{t}^{\tt v}\right>
is

dBang
-meaningful. By

Corollary

11
, for any
u\in\Lambda
,

{\mathtt{F}}^{\tt v}\!\left<{u}^{\tt v}\right>
is

dBang
-meaningful. So, by typing preservation
(
Proposition

18
.
2
)
again,
{(\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>)}^{\tt v}
is

dBang
-meaningful, and hence
\mathtt{F}\!\left<u\right>
is
dCBV
-meaningful using

Theorem

26
.
∎

6
Conclusion and Future Work

We define a notion of meaningful term, in a unifying well-established
framework
dBang
that is able to capture both
CBN
and

CBV
calculi. We validate this notion of meaningfulness by
providing a (high-level) characterization based on both typability and
inhabitation, and showing a (surface) genericity result. Furthermore,
both meaningfulness and genericity in
dBang
are shown to
capture their respective notions in
dCBN
and

dCBV
.

Several questions remain to be explored. First of all, a notion of
stratified reduction, a finer operational semantics generalizing
surface reduction to different levels, has been recently defined for

dCBN
and
dCBV

[
14
]
.
Stratified reduction is a key tool to show a full genericity result
for both strategies. We plan to transfer these techniques to the more
general framework of
dBang
, so that full genericity for

dCBN
and
dCBV
can be simply obtained by
projecting the more general notion of full genericity for

dBang
via
CBN
/
CBV
translations.

We also plan to further study the properties of the theories

\mathcal{H}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}^{*}
) generated by equating all the
meaningful terms (resp. all observational equivalent terms) in

dBang
, which could be possibly related. We believe that
these theories can be related to the corresponding ones in

dCBN
and
dCBV
.

We would like to extend our study to other natural objects in the
theory of programming, such as Böhm trees for
dBang
and
their related theorems (
e.g.
approximation and separability). Böhm
trees for
dBang
are expected to encompass both

dCBN

[
18
]
and

dCBV

[
46
]
ones.

Unifying frameworks such as
dBang
should also provide
other general results for
dCBN
and
dCBV
, such
as standardization, separability, etc. All this is left to future
work. Finally, a more ambitious goal would be to generalize these
results to models of computations with effects, such as global memory,
non-determinism, exceptions, etc. This would approach our study on

dBang
to a more general unifying framework such as
call-by-push-value
[
50
,
51
]
.

Appendix A
Proofs of
Section

2

A.1
Confluence of
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt d}\oc\right>}

Definition 28
.

Let us now extend the reduction relation to contexts. We define the
context reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt d}\oc\right>}
) to be the union of the

\mathrm{F}
-closures of the relations

{\tt dB}_{1},{\tt dB}_{2}
and
{\tt dB}_{3}
(resp.
{\tt d}\oc_{1}
and
{\tt d}\oc_{2}
) defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.\mathtt{F}\right>u&\mapsto_{{\tt dB}_{1}}&\mathtt{L}\!\left<\mathtt{F}[x\backslash u]\right>\\
\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>\mathtt{F}&\mapsto_{{\tt dB}_{2}}&\mathtt{L}\!\left<t[x\backslash\mathtt{F}]\right>\\
\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>[y\backslash\mathtt{F}]\right>u&\mapsto_{{\tt dB}_{3}}&\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<t[x\backslash u]\right>[y\backslash\mathtt{F}]\right>\\[5.69046pt]
{\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{L}\!\left<\oc\mathtt{F}\right>\right)&\mapsto_{{\tt d}\oc_{1}}&\mathtt{L}\!\left<\mathtt{F}\right>\\
{\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<\oc s\right>[x\backslash\mathtt{F}]\right>\right)&\mapsto_{{\tt d}\oc_{2}}&\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<s\right>[x\backslash\mathtt{F}]\right>\end{array}

Lemma 29
.

Let
\mathtt{F}^{1},\mathtt{F}^{2}

be two contexts such that
\mathtt{F}^{1}\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}\mathtt{F}^{2}
for some

\mathcal{R}\in\{{\tt dB},{\tt d}\oc\}
. Then, for any term
t\in\Lambda_{\oc}
, one has that
\mathtt{F}^{1}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}\mathtt{F}^{2}\!\left<t\right>
.

Proof.

We distinguish two cases:

\mathcal{R}={\tt dB}
: By definition, there exist three
contexts
\mathtt{F},\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}
and

\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}
such that

\mathtt{F}^{1}=\mathtt{F}\!\left<\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\right>
,

\mathtt{F}^{2}=\mathtt{F}\!\left<\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\right>
and

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\mapsto_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}
for some
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\in\{{\tt dB}_{1},{\tt dB}_{2},{\tt dB}_{3}\}
. We
distinguish three cases:

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}={\tt dB}_{1}
: Then

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}=\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\right>u
and

\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}=\mathtt{L}\!\left<\mathtt{F}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]\right>
. Thus

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>=\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\!\left<t\right>\right>u

and
\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>=\mathtt{L}\!\left<\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\!\left<t\right>[x\backslash u]\right>
.
Therefore
\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>

hence
\mathtt{F}^{1}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\mathtt{F}^{2}\!\left<t\right>
.

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}={\tt dB}_{2}
: Then

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}=\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>\mathtt{F}^{\prime}
and

\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}=\mathtt{L}\!\left<t[x\backslash\mathtt{F}^{\prime}]\right>
. Thus

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>=\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\!\left<t\right>

and
\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>=\mathtt{L}\!\left<t[x\backslash\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\!\left<t\right>]\right>
.
Therefore
\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>

hence
\mathtt{F}^{1}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\mathtt{F}^{2}\!\left<t\right>
.

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}={\tt dB}_{3}
: Then

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}=\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>[y\backslash\mathtt{F}^{\prime}]\right>u

and
\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}=\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<t[x\backslash u]\right>[y\backslash\mathtt{F}^{\prime}]\right>
.
Thus
\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>=\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>[y\backslash\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\!\left<t\right>]\right>u

and
\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>=\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<t[x\backslash u]\right>[y\backslash\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\!\left<t\right>]\right>
.
Therefore
\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>

hence
\mathtt{F}^{1}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\mathtt{F}^{2}\!\left<t\right>
.

\mathcal{R}={\tt d}\oc
: By definition, there exist three
contexts
\mathtt{F},\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}
and

\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}
such that

\mathtt{F}^{1}=\mathtt{F}\!\left<\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\right>
,

\mathtt{F}^{2}=\mathtt{F}\!\left<\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\right>
and

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\mapsto_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}
for some
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\in\{{\tt d}\oc_{1},{\tt d}\oc_{2}\}
.

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}={\tt d}\oc_{1}
: Then

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}={\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{L}\!\left<\oc\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\right>\right)
and

\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}=\mathtt{L}\!\left<\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\right>
. Thus

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>={\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{L}\!\left<\oc\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\!\left<t\right>\right>\right)
and

\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>=\mathtt{L}\!\left<\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\!\left<t\right>\right>
. Therefore

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>\mapsto_{{\tt d}\oc}\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>
hence

\mathtt{F}^{1}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\mathtt{F}^{2}\!\left<t\right>
.

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}={\tt d}\oc_{2}
: Then

\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}={\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<\oc s\right>[x\backslash\mathtt{F}^{\prime}]\right>\right)
and

\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}=\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<s\right>[x\backslash\mathtt{F}^{\prime}]\right>
.
Thus
\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>={\tt der}\!\left(\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<\oc s\right>[x\backslash\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\!\left<t\right>]\right>\right)
and

\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>=\mathtt{L}_{1}\!\left<\mathtt{L}_{2}\!\left<s\right>[x\backslash\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\!\left<t\right>]\right>
.
Therefore
\mathtt{F}^{1^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>\mapsto_{{\tt d}\oc}\mathtt{F}^{2^{\prime}}\!\left<t\right>

hence
\mathtt{F}^{1}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\mathtt{F}^{2}\!\left<t\right>
.
∎

Lemma 30
.

The reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<{\tt d}\oc\right>}
is diamond.

Proof.

Let
t,u_{1},u_{2}\in\Lambda_{\oc}
such that
u_{1}\neq u_{2}
with
t\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<\mathcal{R}_{1}\right>}u_{1}
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<\mathcal{R}_{2}\right>}u_{2}
for some
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}\in\{{\tt dB},{\tt d}\oc\}
. Let us show that there exists
s\in\Lambda_{\oc}
such that
u_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<\mathcal{R}_{2}\right>}s
and

u_{2}\rightarrow_{\mathrm{F}\!\left<\mathcal{R}_{1}\right>}s
. We distinguish two cases:

\mathcal{R}_{1}={\tt dB}
: By definition, there exist

\mathtt{F}
,
\mathtt{L}
,
s_{1},s_{2}\in\Lambda_{\oc}
such that
t=\mathtt{F}\!\left<\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.s_{1}\right>s_{2}\right>

and
u_{1}=\mathtt{F}\!\left<\mathtt{L}\!\left<s_{1}[x\backslash s_{2}]\right>\right>
. Since

u_{1}\neq u_{2}
, then the step
